# Overview and conﬁrmatory and exploratory factor analysis of AI literacy scale

## Martin J. ## A R T I C L E I N F O A B S T R A C T

## _Keywords:_ Comprehensive concepts of AI literacy (AIL) and valid measures are essential for research (e.g., intervention Artiﬁcial intelligence literacy studies) and practice (e.g., personnel selection/development) alike. To date, several scales have been published, Latent dimensions sharing standard features but diﬀering in some aspects. We ﬁrst aim to brieﬂy overview instruments identiﬁed 

## ConﬁrmatoryExploratory factor factor analysis analysis fromitems. unsystematicWe describe literaturethe instruments research and in compare February them. 2023. We We identiﬁed identiﬁed common four scales themes and and one overlaps collection in theof 

instruments and developmental procedure. We also found diﬀerences regarding scale development procedures and latent dimensions. Following this literature research, we came to the conclusion that the literature on AI literacy measurement was fragmented, and little eﬀort was undertaken to integrate diﬀerent AI literacy conceptualizations. The second focus of this study is to test the factorial structures of existing AIL measurement instruments and identify latent dimensions of AIL across all instruments. We used robust maximum-likelihood conﬁrmatory factor analysis to test factorial structures in a joint survey of all AIL items in an English-speaking online sample (_N_=219). We found general support for all instruments’ factorial structures with minor deviations from the original factorial structures for some of the instruments. In a second analysis step, to address the issue of fragmented research on AI literacy conceptualization and measurement, we used principal axis exploratory factor analysis with oblique rotation to identify latent dimensions across all items. We found four correlated latent dimensions of AIL, which were mostly interpretable as the abilities to use and interact with AI, to design/program AI (incl. in-depth technical knowledge), to perform complex cognitive operations regarding AI (e.g., ethical considerations), and a common factor for the abilities to detect AI/diﬀerentiate between AI and humans and manage persuasive inﬂuences of AI (i.e., persuasion literacy). Our ﬁndings sort the multitude of AIL instruments and reveal four latent core dimensions of AIL. Thus, they contribute importantly to the conceptual understanding 

of AIL that has been fragmented so far.

AI working groups/workshops) to participate in a Delphi procedure. In three rounds, between 53 and 59 participants were asked to rate the rel- evance of the previously generated items and collect items/topics that are still missing. So far, no test regarding the factorial validity has been performed. A majority of the generated items contain wording such as “I can explain...” (_n_ = 

17. and “I can describe...” (_n_ = 11). These items may not only or not primarily capture skills and knowledge concerning AI but also the didac- tic competence to communicate and teach these skills and knowledge. The authors themselves write that the participants of the Delphi study come from the scientiﬁc and higher education sector, and the items may, therefore, only be suitable to a lesser extent for application in the busi- ness context.

### _2.2._ _Comparison_ _of_ _AI_ _literacy_ _instruments_

The ﬁrst thing that stands out in the overview of all instruments is that the researchers who developed them come from diﬀerent aca- demic disciplines (e.g., medical education, engineering, economics/in- formation systems, psychology, and human-computer interaction). It can be favorably concluded that AI literacy is a topic that is cur- rently highly relevant and is viewed from many diﬀerent perspec- tives.

It needs to be mentioned that some of the instruments are not directly based on an AI literacy concept but were only inspired by them (Laupich- ler et al.,[ 2023b;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Pinski & Benlian, 2023). Other instruments neglect the existing research on AI literacy conceptualizations (Long & Magerko, [2020;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Ng et al.,[ 2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) by referring to their authors’ concept of AI liter- acy (Wang et al., 2022; Karaca et al.,[ 2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). Another more positive point worth mentioning seems to be that most researchers asked external ex- perts to review their initially generated items (Karaca et al.,[ 2021;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Wang et al., 2022; Pinski & Benlian, 2023; Laupichler et al.,[ 2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). However, the scope of the expert reviews diﬀered strongly. While some researchers resorted to larger samples of experts for initial item generation (Karaca et al.,[ 2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) or multi-stage Delphi-approaches for item review (Laupich- ler et al.,[ 2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-), other instruments were only reviewed by a selected few experts (Wang et al., 2022; Pinski & Benlian, 2023) or no external experts at all (Carolus et al.,[ 2023)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). All AI literacy instruments have been validated concerning their factorial structure. Here, too, the methodol- ogy and sample sizes diﬀer. Karaca et al. ([2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-)andWang et al. (2022) used exploratory and conﬁrmatory factor analysis in larger samples to identify and conﬁrm the factorial structure. Carolus et al. ([2023)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-)used conﬁrmatory factor analysis in a medium-sized sample and modiﬁed the model. However, they did not collect data in a second sample to conﬁrm the adjusted model. Pinski and Benlian (2023)alsoused conﬁrmatory factor analysis and adjusted their model without additional data collec- tion. However, their sample appears to be quite small with only _n_ = 50, which might lead to an unstable factor solution that is highly dependent on the sample. Laupichler et al. ([2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) decided to publish their item collection without factorial validation.

Overall, the instruments show a clear overlap of dimensions (e.g., ethics, using AI, creating/developing AI, detecting/identifying AI). All instruments include items on the evaluation of strengths/weaknesses. However, in the case of Pinskis and Benlian’s instrument (Pinski & Ben- lian, 2023), these are limited only to the comparison with human actors (e.g., “aspects human actors handle better/worse than AI”). Also com- mon are items related to ethical standards and considerations. While some instruments explicitly refer to the ability/motivation to comply with ethical standards (Karaca et al., [2021;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Wang et al., 2022) or re- fer to the ability to make ethical considerations (Carolus et al.,[ 2023;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Laupichler et al.,[ 2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-), this aspect is neglected in some instruments (Pinski & Benlian, 2023). Most instruments include items on the techni- cal knowledge of AI, with the instrument by Wang et al. (2022)beingthe exception. The instruments by Karaca et al. ([2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-), Wang et al. (2022), 

and Carolus et al. ([2023)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-)explicitlyinclude items on the ability to use AI, which is not included in the other two instruments resp. item collec- tion (Pinski & Benlian, 2023; Laupichler et al.,[ 2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). Rather, Pinski and Benlian (2023)includeitems on the prior experience of interac- tion with and using AI. Laupichler et al. ([2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) entirely neglects the ability to use AI. Some instruments also include items on the ability to diﬀerentiate between AI and non-AI (Wang et al., 2022; Carolus et al., [2023)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). Lastly, items on the ability to create AI are found only in the in- strument by Carolus et al. ([2023)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). The instrument by Pinski and Benlian (2023) contains items on prior experience with designing or developing AI. Complementary psychological constructs such as problem-solving, emotion regulation, learning, and persuasion literacy are only included by Carolus et al. ([2022)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-), which might be explained by their background in psychology. Thus, it might be concluded that a unifying perspective on AI literacy has not been achieved. Diﬀerent authors refer to diﬀerent AI literacy conceptualizations. While some overlap in the dimensions of AI literacy measures exists, other dimensions of the authors’ conceptu- alizations of AI literacy vary widely.

All instruments have in common that their validity has only been in- vestigated regarding their factorial structure. Further tests of any of the AI Literacy measurement instruments’ validity in regard to the intended use of the test Kane ([2013)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-)havenot been presented so far. So far, the gold standard of validation, calculating a correlation of the newly devel- oped instrument with an already validated instrument, is not possible because no valid test or other measure for AI literacy exists. Future work needs to make sure to ﬁnd suitable validity criteria for AI literacy, such as actual interaction with AI or objective tests.

### _2.3._ _Latest_ _trends_ _in_ _AI_ _literacy_ _measurement_

Since we conducted our unsystematic literature research in Febru- ary 2023, more research has been published on or related to measuring AI literacy. Hornberger et al. ([2023)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-)publishedthe ﬁrst validated test for AI literacy. They developed 57 knowledge single based on the AI literacy conceptualization by Long and Magerko ([2020)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-)andtextbooks that three researchers then reviewed. The scale was validated using item response theory (3-parameter model with a guessing probability of 25%). The ﬁnal test consists of 30 single-choice items and one or- dering task. Tests are normally regarded to be more objective than self-assessment scales. The test, however, can only measure knowledge. Other aspects of AI literacy, like self-eﬃcacy or abilities, are neglected by design.

Ng et al. ([2023)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) analyzed literature on AI literacy and existing AI lit- eracy measures and developed another self-report questionnaire consid- ering AI literacy’s aﬀective, behavioral, cognitive, and ethical aspects. The 32-item self-report scale was validated using expert reviews, in- terviews with secondary students, a pilot study, and a full validation study. The items load on subordinate factors, which load on superor- dinate factors, which load on a general factor for AI literacy, as con- ﬁrmed by conﬁrmatory factor analysis. The instrument is speciﬁcally tailored to measure school students’ learning outcomes after participa- tion in an AI literacy program. Also, in 2023, Laupichler et al. ([2023a)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) developed a self-report measurement instrument using their item col- lection (Laupichler et al., [2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). The ﬁnal instrument, which consists of 31 items, is targeted at measuring AI literacy in non-experts (indi- viduals without formal education in AI). It is also targeted at assessing the eﬀects of AI literacy programs. The instrument, which was devel- oped using exploratory factor analysis, consists of three latent dimen- sions (i.e., technical understanding, critical appraisal, and practical ap- praisal).

In addition to newly developed measurement instruments, exist- ing instruments were translated into new languages. For example, the AI Literacy scale by Wang et al. (2022)wasadapted into Turk- ish (Çelebi et al., 2023). In the debate concerning current AI use cases, (Yilmaz & Karaoglan Yilmaz, 2023) highlight the importance of “prompt literacy” (i.e., the ability to construct useful prompts for AI 

### chatbots) and the ability to consider the output of these chatbots from order. We used the same 11-point Likert scale for all items (“A value an ethical point of view. of 0 means that an ability is not at all or hardly pronounced.” and “A 

### value of 10 means that an ability is very well or almost perfectly pro-

.04, .05), SRMR = .03). All identiﬁed latent dimensions were correlated (Table [4)](#correlations-of-the-latent-dimensions). The item loadings for the exploratory factor analysis can be seen in the appendix (Table [B.7)](#appendix-b-factor-loadings). To assign a variable to a factor, we choose a cut-oﬀ of 0.364 as our sample size is close to 200 (Stevens, 2002).

The ﬁrst factor explained 19% of the variance of all items. 35 items highly loaded (loadings _\>_ .364) on this factor. The majority of these items focused on the use of and interaction with AI and the solving of problems related to the use of AI. The second factor also explained 19% of the variance of all items. 40 items, mostly concerning the ability to design and program AI and in-depth technical knowledge, are highly loaded on the second factor. The third factor explained 19% of the vari- ance of all items included in the analysis as well. 43 items highly loaded on this factor (_\>_ .364). The items that loaded on this factor refer to abil- ities and knowledge that relate to more complex cognitive operations, such as the ability to make ethical considerations in regard to AI, the ability to think about risks and opportunities, as well as strengths and weaknesses of AI, and also includes items on knowledge on AI. The last factor explains 6% of the variance of all items. Eight items show load- ings (_\>_ .364) on this factor. The items are mostly concerned with the detection of AI, diﬀerentiation of AI and humans, and persuasion liter- acy. Only 9 items did not strongly load on any of the four factors (i.e., with a loading _\>_ .364).

### _4.3.4._ _Correlational_ _analysis_ _of_ _latent_ _dimensions_ _identiﬁed_ _from_ _all_ _AI_ _literacy_ _scales_ _and_ _additional_ _measures_

We calculated Pearson correlations to identify how the latent di- mensions we identiﬁed related to the additional measures (Table [5)](#correlations-of-the-latent-dimensions). Please note that these correlations are based on a much smaller sample (_n_ = 141) because not all individuals ﬁlled out the additional measures. We used factor scores from the exploratory factor analysis (method = tenBerge) and averages across the items for the additional measures. Positive attitude towards AI was related to the ﬁrst three factors but not the fourth factor. The negative attitude was negatively correlated with the ﬁrst factor but was unrelated to the other factors of AI liter- acy. The pragmatic motive was related to Factor I (i.e., interaction with AI and solving problems related to AI) and Factor III (i.e., abilities and knowledge related to more complex cognitive operations). Eudaimonic motives were related to Factors I and III as well. Hedonic motives were

5. **Discussion**

The ﬁrst goal of the current study was to provide an overview of ex- isting AI literacy measurement instruments to aid researchers and prac- titioners in selecting a suitable instrument. Until now, deciding which instrument to use to measure AI literacy was diﬃcult. This literature overview contributed to the research by providing an overview of the instruments we identiﬁed in our literature research. The overview in the related work showed that the instruments overlap but also have dif- ferences. This is true not only for the formulation and contents of the items and the factorial structures but also for the background of the re- searchers, the AI literacy concepts, and other frameworks that guided the instruments, as well as the methodology for item development and validation. Diﬀerent aspects can be included in the selection of an in- strument by researchers and practitioners: Most of the instruments are validated regarding their factorial structure except the item collection by Laupichler et al. ([2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). Similarly, the factorial structure of the in- strument by Pinski and Benlian (Pinski & Benlian, 2023) was only tested in a small sample. As instruments diﬀer regarding the exact dimensions of AI literacy, researchers and practitioners can select an instrument that ﬁts their particular needs in terms of the constructs of interest. Most but not all researchers asked experts to review their items (see also sec- tion [2.2)](#22-comparison-of-ai-literacy-instruments).

All of the measurement instruments are self-eﬃcacy scales, which do not measure the actual ability of the users but rather the individual’s belief that they are AI literate (i.e., self-eﬃcacy). This might be helpful and even more important for some measurement purposes (e.g.). A test for AI Literacy would oﬀer the beneﬁt of making the external validation of AI literacy self-assessment scales possible. Most existing approaches that use a knowledge test (e.g., see Long et al.,[ 2021;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Ali et al.,[ 2019;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Ng et al., [2022;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Kong et al., [2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) are very speciﬁcally tailored to a certain context or to evaluate knowledge gain of speciﬁc lecture mate- rial (e.g., concerning speciﬁc machine learning algorithms). This makes them unsuitable for measuring general AI knowledge in a broad popu- lation. The test by Hornberger et al. ([2023)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-)mightbe more suitable to validate AI literacy self-report questionnaires as it was developed inde- pendently from a speciﬁc intervention.

Second, we tested the factorial structure of the instruments. In con- ﬁrmatory factor analysis, we found mostly support for the factorial structures of the measurement instruments. For one model, no devia- tions can be found at all (Karaca et al.,[ 2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) while there are indicators for slight (Wang et al., 2022; Carolus et al.,[ 2023)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) or stronger deviations (Pinski & Benlian, 2023) from the factorial structures presented in the original publications. Overall, it seems likely that the present data sup- ports the original factorial structures. In principle, variations concerning the factorial models between diﬀerent samples do not argue against the originally identiﬁed factorial structure of the measurement instruments. Rather, diﬀerences between samples (e.g., diﬀerent skill levels, diﬀer- ent prior experiences, diﬀerent attitudes, and diﬀerent interests) might lead to measurement non-invariance (i.e., diﬀerent functioning of items in diﬀerent samples, (Putnick & Bornstein, 2016)). Overall, diﬀerent AI literacy dimensions can be found depending on the items used. Put dif- ferently, it is possible to ﬁnd the structure you expect to ﬁnd if you use items that were developed following the expected structure. Identifying one true conception of AI literacy and falsifying the other conceptions is impossible. Using exploratory factor analysis, we also identiﬁed a two- factorial structure with a good ﬁt for the item collection by Laupichler et al. ([2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). The items loading on the ﬁrst factor mainly refer to complex cognitive operations about AI (i.e., diﬀerentiation of AI and human in- telligence, ethical considerations, risks, opportunities, and strengths and weaknesses). The second factor is comprised of items that refer to more in-depth technical knowledge of AI, machine learning, and data science. This factorial structure of the items by Laupichler et al. ([2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-)partly parallels the results of the exploratory factor analysis we used to identify latent dimensions across the items from all measurement instruments. We found four factors across all instruments. The ﬁrst factor is com- prised of items on the use of and interaction with AI and the solving of problems related to the use of AI. The second factor includes items on the ability to design and program AI and in-depth technical knowl- edge. The third factor is comprised of items related to more complex cognitive operations (i.e., detection of AI and diﬀerentiation of AI and human intelligence, persuasion literacy, ethical considerations, and con- sideration of risks, opportunities, strengths, and weaknesses). The fourth factor includes items that relate to the abilities to detect AI, diﬀerentiate between AI and humans, and manage own information (i.e., persua- sion literacy). This result is, ﬁrst and foremost, very encouraging, as it clearly shows that there are central dimensions of AI literacy that can be found across instruments. Thus, latent dimensions can be seen and understood as AI literacy. In their entirety, however, the identi- ﬁed factors do not correspond to any of the assumed conceptual models of the instruments used (Karaca et al., [2021;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Wang et al., 2022; Pin- ski & Benlian, 2023; Carolus et al., [2023;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Laupichler et al., [2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). Also, there is no exact correspondence with the most cited AI literacy conceptions Long and Magerko ([2020)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-); Ng et al. ([2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). However, a huge overlap appeared even though the factor analysis across all items shows signiﬁcantly less diﬀerentiated factors than in the individual in- struments and the conceptualizations (Long & Magerko,[ 2020;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Ng et al., [2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-).

The original studies from which AI literacy items were drawn uti- lized diverse methodologies, including varying population demograph- ics, contexts, and conceptual frameworks. As can be seen in table [1,](#overview-of-ai-literacy-scales) the development of the AI literacy items that were used in this factor anal- ysis varies widely. Some item collections were only developed by the authors, and others also asked experts about their opinions on useful items to measure AI literacy. The sample sizes for validation, number of items, and original types of Likert scales utilized are highly diverse. Also, the AI literacy conceptualizations used to inspire item creation are diﬀerent: Ng et al. ([2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) included a separate factor for AI ethics and a joint factor of evaluation and creation of AI, whereas our factor analysis showed a joint factor including ethical considerations and evaluations of AI, and another joint factor of deeper technical knowledge and the abil- ity to create AI. Their dimension of the ability to use and apply AI is very similar to our ﬁrst factor (i.e., the ability to use and interact with AI). 

As various information sources were used to develop the totality of all items, it seems unsurprising that none of the individual factorial models is identical to the overall model. The items were developed by indepen- dent groups of researchers who referred to diﬀerent conceptualizations and used diﬀerent methods for item development. It seems impossible to trace exactly which methodological decisions and circumstances of the context and the sample had an eﬀect on the developed items and how. However, the stability and validity of the identiﬁed dimensions are strongly dependent on many methodological decisions made by the authors of the items, so the general validity and applicability of the iden- tiﬁed dimensions can be questioned.

An interesting approach could be to further develop the identiﬁed factors and associated items into a comprehensive measurement in- strument for AI literacy. This measurement instrument would be less dependent on the measurement interests and intended target groups of the authors of the individual AI literacy measurement instruments. A uniﬁed measurement instrument for AI literacy could be broadly ap- plicable for assessing AI literacy in diverse educational, commercial, and research settings. Alternatively, a modular approach would be pos- sible where a researcher or practitioner selects the items most appro- priate for their research goals. So far, however, the collection of items is too long to be used for measuring AI literacy in research and prac- tice. Additionally, conﬁrmation of the dimensions in an independent sample is necessary. Therefore, further validation steps and shorten- ing of the item collection along the identiﬁed dimensions are neces- sary. It seems especially important to conﬁrm the identiﬁed dimension in an independent sample. Until then, using one of the validated in- struments selected for a speciﬁc measurement purpose remains prefer- able.

The correlations between the four factors suggest that there may be a superordinate factor for overall AI literacy. Correlations of the fac- tors with additional measures suggest that individuals with a positive attitude towards AI also show higher AI literacy, which might not be surprising. However, this is not true for Factor IV (i.e., the ability to detect AI and manage information). More interestingly, negative atti- tudes towards AI were unrelated to any of the four latent dimensions of AI literacy. Further, individuals who tend to seek clarity when using technology (pragmatic motive) and tend to use technology to become their best selves (eudaimonic motive) also show higher AI literacy (prag- matic motive is only related to factors I and III). Presumably, individuals with a more positive attitude towards AI, a more pragmatic approach to technology, and a higher interest in improving themselves using tech- nology are more likely to use AI and thus show higher AI literacy. The non-signiﬁcant correlations might be explained by a lack of relationship between the constructs. As research on AI literacy is still in an early stage of maturity, little is known about the relationship of AI literacy dimensions to other constructs. Overall, the motives for using technol- ogy seem less strongly related to the in-depth knowledge and detection of AI/persuasion literacy. More research will be needed to identify im- portant correlates of AI literacy.

### _5.1._ _Limitations_

Some limitations regarding the present empirical study should be mentioned. During data collection, the questionnaire server was oﬄine for several hours, so the survey remained incomplete for some par- ticipants. For another part of the participants, all data was lost. The participants had the opportunity to ﬁll in the questionnaire a second time. Individuals were probably less motivated due to these technical issues and increased eﬀort. To mitigate this disadvantage, participants who completed the questionnaire twice were also compensated twice. Overall, the number of participants appears to be small for such a high number of items across all instruments. For 134 items on AI literacy, there are only 219 participants. A small sample might be problem- atic because the limited number of observations may lead to unstable 

factor solutions and unreliable identiﬁed latent dimensions, as well as increased susceptibility to sampling variability.

The sample is this study’s second most limiting factor. It only con- sisted of 219 individuals, which is often considered insuﬃcient for the type of analyses we ran. However, some researchers suggest that even smaller samples can yield stable factor solutions (de Winter et al.,[ 2009)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-). In addition to the size, the sample is limited as participants mostly came from the United Kingdom. The transferability of the results to other countries can, therefore, be questioned. A transfer of the results might be especially problematic concerning non-English speaking regions and populations.

It could be criticized that we used both exploratory and conﬁrmatory factor analysis in one study. This is contrary to the classical approach where a sample is split in two halves to develop and test a measure- ment model. However, this criticism falls short for the current study: The models that we tested for the measurement instruments of () are independent of the models that we have newly developed (the model by (Laupichler et al., [2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-)andthe overall model). However, we ac- knowledge the need to test the newly developed model in an indepen- dent sample.

We used one identical 11-point Likert scale for all AIL measures to avoid confusing the participants. As argued by Juniper ([2009)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-), vali- dated questionnaires should not be modiﬁed as this might impact the scale’s validity. In our case, this might have aﬀected the applicability of the ﬁndings to the original versions of the scales. Diﬀerences in the conﬁrmatory factor loadings for some of the scales might be explained thereby, and results of the exploratory factor loadings might not apply if the original version of the scales is used. However, we do not consider this problem to be too great, as most of the factorial structures of the instruments were reproduced.

Further, it needs to be mentioned that we did not conduct a system- atic review of AIL instruments and did not follow PRISMA guidelines for our literature research. Thus, the transparency and reproducibility of this overview might be limited. However, due to the small number of publications on AIL measuring instruments, we consider this prob- lem minor. The instruments included in our empirical study are only a snapshot of the current AI literacy measures. It is unclear whether one or a few instruments will prevail and become the standard for measur- ing AI literacy or whether various possible instruments will be used in parallel. As new instruments and other empirical works are published and our understanding of AI interaction changes, AI literacy’s focus and dimensions might change.

Similarly, the additional measures we chose can be questioned in the context of AI literacy validation. While the instruments for atti- tude towards AI (Schepman & Rodway, 2020)andanthropomorphism, (Neave et al., [2015)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-)werepeer-reviewed and used regularly, the instru- ment for motives has not been peer-reviewed and must be considered more experimental. Selecting suitable variables for divergent and con- vergent validity is diﬃcult because little is known about AI literacy and its correlates. Thus, the inclusion of these speciﬁc variables per se can be questioned.

### _5.2._ _Future_ _research_

A systematic review following PRISMA guidelines (Page et al., 2021) should be conducted on AIL measures ensuring a more focused and re- liable synthesis due to its rigorous methodology, comprehensive search strategy, and strict inclusion criteria, Next, empirical work on AI liter- acy measurement should focus on validating the AI literacy instruments. For this purpose, external criteria such as objective tests, external assess- ments (e.g., by supervisors or external experts), or behavioral observa- tions might be useful. So far, we know that all AI literacy instruments measure similar aspects as they are highly correlated. However, it re- mains unclear to date how strongly these measurements are related to actual competence in using and interacting with AI. Subsequently, a comparison of the instruments in terms of their predictive validity would 

be interesting to determine the value the diﬀerent instruments bring to the table regarding the prediction.

6. **Conclusion**

First, we provided an overview of existing AI literacy measurement instruments. As more and more AI literacy scales (e.g., Laupichler et al.,[ 2023a;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Ng et al.,[ 2023)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) and other articles concerned with the mea- surement of AI literacy (e.g., Çelebi et al., 2023; Yilmaz & Karaoglan Yilmaz, 2023) are published, it gets more and more diﬃcult to keep an overview. The current article contributes to the literature by giv- ing an overview. Second, we tested the factorial structure of several AI literacy instruments (i.e., Wang et al., 2022; Karaca et al., [2021;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Carolus et al.,[ 2023;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Pinski & Benlian, 2023). As hypothesized, it was possible to conﬁrm the measurement models of diﬀerent measures of AI literacy using conﬁrmatory factor analysis. Third, we identiﬁed la- tent core dimensions across the items of all instruments (Wang et al., 2022; Karaca et al.,[ 2021;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Carolus et al.,[ 2023;](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) Pinski & Benlian, 2023; Laupichler et al., [2023b)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) using exploratory factor analysis. For this purpose, we combined the data on all available AI literacy measure- ment instruments. As hypothesized, it was possible to identify latent dimensions of AI literacy across all items for AI literacy using ex- ploratory factor analysis. We were able to identify the following di- mensions: (I) the ability to use and interact with AI, (II) the ability to design/program AI and in-depth technical knowledge, (III) the abil- ity to perform more complex cognitive operations regarding AI, and 

1. the ability to detect AI, diﬀerentiate between AI and humans, and to manage own information (i.e., persuasion literacy). These abilities include or partly overlap with the AI literacy conceptualizations by Long and Magerko ([2020)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-); Ng et al. ([2021)](#factor-1-factor-factor-factor-h2-bandura-a-2006-guide-for-constructing-self-eﬃcacy-scales-vol--greenwich-ct-infor-) which were developed fol- lowing theoretical considerations and literature review. This, however, is not surprising as several author groups’ AI literacy measures are based on these conceptualizations.

The present work contributes in two ways: First, it helps to under- stand and select a measurement instrument to collect AI literacy data. Second, it advances the conceptual research on AI literacy and prior eﬀorts that were made to conceptualize AI literacy. It is relevant for scientists and practitioners who plan training, interventions, or scien- tiﬁc studies and want to select an AI literacy conceptualization and an instrument that is suitable for their purpose. Future work should fo- cus on establishing criterion validity through objective measures and predictive validity with a focus on incremental validity of individual instruments.
